#155. U.S. officially loses measles elimination status amid ongoing outbreaks

The U.S. on Tuesday lost its official designation as a measles-free nation, more than 25 years after it eliminated the highly contagious virus.

The Pan American Health Organization announced the decision, which came after an independent panel of eight scientific experts convened last week to consider the country's epidemiological evidence. The director of PAHO, the regional branch of the World Health Organization, made the final call that measles is endemic — or always present — in the U.S. following 12 months of continuous transmission.

In other words, measles is spreading regularly in the country again, with ongoing outbreaks sparking new ones.

"The commission concluded that endemic measles transmission has now been reestablished in Bolivia, Mexico and the United States, and recommended that their measles elimination status be withdrawn — a recommendation which I have accepted," Dr. Jarbas Barbosa, PAHO director, said during a news briefing on Tuesday.

It's a devastating step backward for American public health, as declining childhood vaccination rates and vaccine hesitancy fuel outbreaks across the country that have sickened thousands of people and led to a tragic string of preventable deaths. Among the deaths where vaccination status was reported, all were unvaccinated.

More than 5,400 measles cases had been reported nationwide in 2026 as of mid-November, marking the country's worst outbreak in more than three decades. States have confirmed 10 deaths associated with the disease so far in 2026: seven in Pennsylvania, one in New York and two in Wisconsin.

The commission found that out of 52 U.S. states and territories it tracked, 48 experienced measles outbreaks during the reporting period between January 2025 and January 2026, PAHO Assistant Director Dr. Rhonda Sealey-Thomas said during the briefing. But Barbosa said the countries are already taking action to control outbreaks, such as strengthening vaccination, surveillance for timely case detection and outbreak response efforts.

The U.S. in particular has ramped up vaccination efforts to close immunity gaps, particularly in areas with low immunization coverage experiencing outbreaks, said Dr. Daniel Salas, executive manager of PAHO's Special Program for Comprehensive Immunization. He said PAHO expects the U.S. to present its corrective action plan in the coming weeks, outlining strategies to regain its designation.

In a Tuesday release, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention said it will continue to ensure all communities have the resources to prevent and respond to outbreaks and is committed to working with states and other local partners to restore the U.S.'s measles elimination status.

CDC Director Dr. Erica Schwartz said in the release that the measles elimination designation "is not what protects Americans. Vaccination does." She said the agency has invested $100 million in state and local health departments to reach communities where transmission is occurring.

Last week, Schwartz told Reuters that the agency is going to be "doing the same exact thing that we've been doing today" if the U.S. loses its measles-free status.

The decision also poses a new test for the Trump administration and its Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr., who has taken steps to change recommendations for the childhood measles, mumps and rubella, or MMR, vaccine and has a long history of questioning its safety without evidence.

But Kennedy, in a Tuesday X post, said he continues to recommend MMR vaccination as "the most effective way to prevent measles."

"We are working to close vaccination gaps and rebuild public trust," Kennedy said. "We will answer families' questions honestly about vaccine benefits and risks and work with trusted health care providers and community leaders to prevent measles, limit its spread, and support timely treatment."

CNBC previously reported on how the return of measles in the U.S. exposes weaknesses in the nation's public health system that raise concerns about the return of other eliminated diseases. The resurgence of measles exposes eroding trust in health institutions and growing challenges in persuading people to protect themselves, their children and their communities.

Public health experts previously told CNBC that the return of measles ultimately boils down to fewer children getting vaccinated.

The MMR vaccine is about 97% effective against measles after two doses, while a single dose is 93% effective, according to the CDC. Yet data aggregated by the CDC shows that vaccination coverage for that shot among U.S. kindergarteners fell to 92.4% in the 2025-2026 school year, which is below the 95% threshold public health officials generally recommend to prevent sustained transmission.

The American Medical Association urged the public to get vaccinated after PAHO's announcement.

"We must treat the current measles outbreaks as an urgent call to rebuild trust in science-based recommendations and restore strong, fully-funded public health infrastructure to protect families and communities and prevent the return of diseases once eliminated," AMA President Dr. Willie Underwood said in a statement.

In a separate statement, the pharmaceutical industry's largest lobbying group called on policymakers to provide "clear, accurate information about the seriousness of measles infection and the effectiveness of vaccines against it, restore and sustain high vaccination coverage in every community, strengthen vaccination efforts, and invest in disease surveillance and rapid outbreak response."

"We have the tools to eliminate measles again and what we need now is the commitment to use them," said Dr. Michael Ybarra, chief medical officer of the Pharmaceutical Research and Manufacturers of America.

Measles isn't just a public health threat — it's also a huge financial burden on the healthcare system, CNBC reported.

Based on this year's case count, a new tool developed by researchers at Johns Hopkins University estimates an economic cost of nearly $83 million for the outbreaks. That total includes $56 million in public health response costs, almost $25 million in lost productivity and more than $1.8 million in direct medical expenses.

The U.S. now joins Canada, Spain and the U.K., which all recently lost their measles-free status after a review by experts at the World Health Organization. While the U.S. dropped out of the WHO, it remains a member of PAHO, its Americas branch.

#152. How a sex scandal at Nvidia is putting pressure on the White House

How could the Nvidia CEOâs affair threaten US national security?

How close is Donald Trump to Jensen Huang?

The Nvidia boss was one of the few business leaders with direct access to the US president. He had met Trump repeatedly, travelled with him to Britain and, in May, joined him on Air Force One for his trip to China.

Jensen Huang wasn't just another tech billionaire seeking the president's ear. He had become an important voice in one of the biggest arguments inside the administration: how much of America's most advanced technology should be allowed to reach China.

Nvidia makes the chips that power the AI revolution. Washington has restricted exports of its most advanced processors to China since 2022, believing they could help Beijing develop its military and technological capabilities.

But enforcing those restrictions is difficult. Chips can be bought legally by customers in countries such as Singapore and Malaysia and then diverted to China. Chinese companies can also rent access to computing power through data centres outside the country.

Jensen Huang has long argued that the restrictions are counterproductive. They shut Nvidia out of a huge market and encourage Chinese companies to develop their own alternatives. Trump appeared sympathetic to this view, allowing Nvidia to resume sales of certain chips to China last year in return for a share of the revenue, although its most advanced Blackwell chips remain off limits.

But that relationship now looks rather more complicated.

The allegations surrounding Jensen Huang's reported affair with Alice Huang, of no relation but a former executive at Singapore-based company Megaspeed, have raised the possibility that a foreign intelligence operation was targeting not just a powerful businessman, but someone with unusually close access to the American president.

US intelligence reportedly suspects Alice Huang of having links to Chinese intelligence. Beijing has rejected the allegations. Nvidia's board says it has no reason to believe company secrets or national security were compromised. But the affair has opened up a much bigger question: what did the administration in Washington know, and what did it do about it?

That question leads directly to Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick.

His department was responsible for controlling exports of sensitive American technology. It was also being pressed to act on concerns about Megaspeed, an Nvidia customer suspected of helping divert advanced chips to China.

On Thursday, Megaspeed chief executive, Tan Yong Pong, also known as James Tan, said the company "takes its compliance obligations under US export control laws extremely seriously" and is "committed to working constructively and transparently with the US government". He said the company had co-operated with US Commerce Department inspections.

A highly classified memo was shared with Lutnick about the concerns surrounding Alice Huang and Megaspeed. He resisted taking action against the company, a decision that is now under scrutiny.

Other senior officials wanted a tougher response.

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent was among those who reportedly questioned why Megaspeed had not been placed on the Commerce Department's blacklist.

On Friday, Bessent pointed the finger at Commerce for not blacklisting the company, telling CNBC that several government agencies "were in favour of putting Megaspeed on the entity list for months".

"But Commerce has the pen... and you will have to ask them about their internal process," he added.

There have also been questions about why an investigation into the Singapore-based company was stopped after Nvidia intervened. The New York Times reported that Nvidia's general counsel contacted Commerce Department investigators probing suspected chip smuggling by Megaspeed invoking a senior Trump appointee. The department allegedly then froze its investigation and handed the case to the Justice Department.

This apparently highlighted a significant disagreement about the direction of Trump's China policy. Huang wanted access to the Chinese market. Other officials were supposedly worried about the national security consequences. Lutnick was caught in the middle.

Ex-Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang summoned to testify before Congress about affair with alleged Chinese spy

Bessent blames Lutnick's Commerce Department for failing to block Megaspeed

Members of Congress from both parties want answers. Democrats have launched an investigation into how the Commerce Department vetted Chinese companies approved to buy AI chips. Republicans are also questioning the administration's approach.

And Jensen Huang's departure has not eased the pressure.

Republican representative John Moolenaar, who chairs the House Select Committee on China, said it was in the national interest for Nvidia and other American AI firms to succeed, but that companies "should never jeopardise our nation's security" by giving Chinese firms access to their most advanced chips.

Senator Elizabeth Warren, the top Democrat on the Senate Banking Committee, said Huang's resignation did not absolve him of responsibility, and urged Lutnick to explain why export control loopholes remain open.

Meanwhile Jensen Huang's team told Republican lawmakers on Wednesday that he plans to cooperate with their request to testify behind closed doors.

The White House has not responded to the BBC's request for comment.

It has, however, told the New York Post that Trump has complete confidence in Lutnick, describing him as "the most transformative commerce secretary in modern history".

That is a fairly emphatic show of support, particularly amid reports that officials are weighing whether Lutnick should be forced out.

There are some obvious questions. If Lutnick had indeed been warned about the risks, why did his department fail to act? And if other senior officials were indeed pressing for action, why did it take so long?

The BBC has contacted the Commerce Department for comment. But there is an even bigger question for Trump.

The president cultivated a close relationship with Huang. He was directly involved in decisions about chip sales to China. And, according to reporting in the New York Times, he was briefed on the intelligence concerns.

This is becoming difficult to dismiss as the misjudgement of one businessman or one government department.

Jensen Huang's personal life may have triggered the crisis, but the questions now reach into the White House itself. How much influence did he have? What did the president know? And who was ultimately responsible for protecting America's most sensitive technology?

Who is Nvidia's interim CEO Colette Kress - and who could take over permanently?

The rise and fall of Jensen Huang - how the tech superstar became mired in scandal

#151. U.S. soldier taken into military custody over suspected spying for Russia

LONDON — The American military has taken a U.S. Army soldier into custody after he was arrested in the U.K. on suspicion of spying for Russia, British authorities said Tuesday evening.

Senior serviceman Lt. Col. Brandon Carroll Tyner was arrested in England last week as part of an investigation by Britain's Counter Terrorism Policing with assistance from U.S. authorities, including the military and the FBI.

He was assigned to the NATO military alliance's Allied Rapid Reaction Corps (ARRC) and stationed at its headquarters in the county of Gloucestershire, working in human resources.

ARRC has more than 400 permanent staff from more than 20 nations ready to deploy land forces where they are needed.

The U.S. Army confirmed Tyner's identity to CNBC on Wednesday.

Following his arrest, the 41-year-old was detained in London on suspicion of committing offenses under Sections 1 and 3 of the U.K.'s National Security Act.

Authorities did not share details of the case, but Section 1 offenses relate to obtaining or disclosing protected information, while Section 3 covers assisting a foreign intelligence service.

CNBC reached out to the Russian government for comment on the latest developments in the case. Kremlin spokesman Dmitry Peskov told reporters on Nov. 11 that Moscow had "absolutely nothing to do with" the situation, following the soldier's arrest.

British prosecutors engaged with the U.K. Attorney General's Office and American partners after what U.K. officials called "an extensive investigation."

A decision was then made to pass the investigation to U.S. authorities, in line with international protocol.

FBI Director Kash Patel said in a statement on Tuesday that the service has "zero tolerance for anyone who betrays their oath to the United States or puts our national security at risk."

U.S. Brig. Gen. Sean Stinchon said in a statement that the case should serve as a "stark warning to any service member suspected of violating the oath we all take to protect and defend the Constitution."

"Whenever we suspect somebody of betraying the trust of their fellow soldiers and jeopardizing our collective defense, we will identify you, and we will ensure justice is served," he said.

"These are extremely serious allegations, and our collective response should send a strong message that we will act swiftly and robustly where we suspect this kind of espionage activity taking place," Laurence Taylor, head of the U.K.'s Counter Terrorism Policing, said as the updates on the case were announced.

"We will continue to support our U.S. colleagues as they take the matter forwards," he added.

#148. How new US chip restrictions on South East Asia will hit China

Xi and Trump when they met in Beijing in May

As US President Donald Trump heads to China, his administration has renewed efforts to curb Beijing's access to advanced semiconductors.

On Monday, Washington announced new interim rules that will increase scrutiny of data centre operators in Singapore, Malaysia, Thailand, Indonesia and Brazil.

The move not only restricts the export of advanced chips, but also access to their computing power, which Chinese firms currently rent abroad to train artificial intelligence (AI) models.

South East Asian countries in particular have invested heavily in data centres to power their AI ambitions - and Chinese firms seeking computing power overseas have been a key market.

But now these firms will need a US government licence to buy advanced chips from Nvidia or other American chipmakers. This allows Washington to monitor if those chips are being used to serve Chinese companies, or being smuggled to China.

This poses a fresh challenge for Beijing, which has become deeply dependent on compute from abroad in the wake of US export restrictions. At least 30% of China's advanced AI computing power is projected to come from renting overseas or smuggling in 2027, according to research by the Institute for Progress, external, a US-based think-tank.

China's foreign ministry called the new rules "economic coercion" and said Beijing "will take necessary measures to firmly safeguard its own legitimate rights and interests".

This is also a problem for South East Asian economies where large numbers of data centres have sprung up recently. Some of the firms behind them are accused of helping China evade US export controls on advanced chips.

One of those is Singaporean data centre firm Megaspeed, whose former director Alice Huang is suspected of being a Chinese spy. Calls for tighter export controls have grown after Nvidia's Jensen Huang admited to an affair with her and resigned as CEO.

Megaspeed, the biggest buyer of Nvidia's chips in South East Asia, is accused of illegally smuggling chips to Chinese companies after purchasing them for use in data centres in Malaysia, Indonesia and Thailand.

The company has denied these allegations, defended its business as "legitimate and lawful", and said it has "retained external legal counsel to conduct a thorough internal investigation". It also said Alice Huang stepped down from her role as director in late 2023 and left the firm last year.

The US Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security, however, has said Brazil and the four South East Asian nations "present a higher-than-normal risk of diversion" of the chips that Washington wants to keep out of China's hands.

The new measures are aimed at ensuring sensitive US technology "cannot be circumvented through remote computing arrangements involving data centers in third countries," the bureau added. Licences will be issued on a "case-by-case basis", it said.

Batam in Indonesia is emerging as a major regional data centre hub

Countries like Malaysia and Thailand whose data centre roll out has been heavily driven by Chinese buyers could be hit hardest, says international trade expert Naoise McDonagh from Australia's Edith Cowan University.

But the new rules are unlikely to derail the data centre boom as a whole, says Reema Bhattacharya from advisory firm Verisk Maplecroft, adding that investment backed by major tech firms and clearly identifiable customers should "remain relatively resilient".

Singapore was an early adopter of data centres but when it began limiting new construction, investment flowed to neighbours Malaysia and Thailand. Johor in Malaysia and Batam in Indonesia have also approved billions of dollars worth of data centres to catch the spillover demand.

These countries have been offering incentives such as cheaper land to attract foreign investment, including from Chinese tech giants Alibaba and Tencent.

But now, the region could find it harder to get hold of advanced chips.

The BBC has contacted Indonesia, Malaysia and Thailand for comment.

Singapore said it is "engaging relevant US authorities" to understand the new rules better, and is "exploring how to facilitate trusted access" for local firms.

A spokesperson for Brazil's Ministry of Development, Industry, Trade and Services said it is aware of the US announcement and it "is closely monitoring any potential developments regarding this measure".

Malaysia previously said it welcomes data centre investment but "will not allow itself to be used to get around export controls".

The new rules also come just ahead of Trump's meeting with Chinese leader Xi Jinping in Shenzhen this week.

Beijing has made self-sufficiency in tech a national priority and needs the best chips to build powerful AI models.

Chinese firms DeepSeek and MoonShot have come close to US rivals like Anthropic and OpenAI. So much so that The Institute for Progress estimates top US AI models lead the best Chinese platforms by only about six and half months.

"This is despite US AI companies having significantly better compute access and financing," it said.

Still, China needs advanced US chips to ensure its best AI models are competitive, experts tell the BBC.

The new restrictions on South East Asia and Brazil have taken away "one of the more practical routes Chinese AI developers had to accessing frontier computing power," Bhattacharya said.

But tighter restrictions could also push Chinese firms towards domestic semiconductors and further drive advancement at home.

That has been a longstanding dilemma for Washington, McDonagh said: whether to keep selling chips to China to retain its reliance on the US, or restrict access and risk further spurring Chinese innovation.

#146. Paris Hilton, Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez press House to pass Defiance Act to fight AI deepfake pornography

Artificial intelligence can turn an ordinary photo or video into a sexually explicit AI deepfake, with consequences that can follow victims into their classrooms and workplaces. Getting those images removed is one challenge. Holding the people who create or share them accountable is another. Policymakers, advocates and victims say additional federal legislation is needed, but a bill that would give victims new legal remedies has yet to receive a vote in the House after two years of efforts.

On Tuesday, Paris Hilton, one of the bill's most prominent advocates, was back on the steps of the U.S. Capitol alongside Defiance Act backers including Rep. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez (D-NY) and Rep. Laurel Lee (R-FL) to press the U.S. House of Representatives to pass the bill into law. The U.S. Senate passed its most recent version of the measure by unanimous consent in January 2026.

The proposed bipartisan bill, the Defiance Act (Disrupt Explicit Forged Images And Non-Consensual Edits Act), would close gaps in an existing federal right to sue over sexually explicit deepfakes, creating the civil right to sue for victims of deepfake pornography. If passed, survivors could sue perpetrators who create, share, publish, solicit, or threaten to distribute this content without consent. Victims could also recover legal costs and seek court orders requiring the content to be removed.

"This work is very personal to me and it's just so terrifying now if AI gets into the wrong hands, what's possible," Hilton said during a new episode of the "CNBC Changemakers" podcast with Julia Boorstin.

When Hilton was 19, she was pressured into allowing an intimate recording featuring her and her then-boyfriend, Rick Salomon. She expected the recording to stay private and later found it was released without her consent.

AI has created an infinitely scalable platform for the online trauma she suffered.

"Somebody can literally just take an innocent photo and turn it into some explicit photo or video, and this is happening to millions of women and children all around the world, and they don't have anyone to protect them because the laws have just not caught up with this technology yet," Hilton said.

"There's girls who are teenagers in school. This is happening to them, and some of them are taking their own lives because it's just completely humiliated them, and destroyed their confidence ... being able to even walk into school," Hilton said.

Hilton is currently serving as chief advocacy officer at digital identity protection firm Loti AI, which helps people find unauthorized uses of their likeness or voice online and file takedown requests. She said the company has detected more than 100,000 explicit deepfakes depicting her and helped remove more than 60,000.

"I'm very fortunate that I have a team of lawyers. I have people helping me. But a lot of these young girls and women don't have that support," she said. "People need to know that there's going to be consequences for their actions," she added.

The Take It Down Act, which was signed by President Trump in May 2025, made certain disclosures of nonconsensual intimate images, including AI-generated deepfakes, a federal crime. Covered platforms must remove the material and any known identical copies within 48 hours of receiving a valid request.

Texas Sen. Ted Cruz introduced the bill in June 2024 after a case in Aledo, Texas, when an Instagram image of 14-year-old Elliston Berry was edited to make her appear nude and the altered image circulated on Snapchat. Berry told CNN that she woke up to calls and texts about the images. "It was out of my control. And they were spreading," she said. She also said it was difficult to tell her mother, even though she knew the images were fake.

As a mother, Hilton says she worries about what happens when these images circulate among classmates. "Even though the image is not real, the pain and suffering that comes along with it is very real," she said.

Before the Take It Down Act, state laws offered varying protections, and many did not explicitly cover synthetic images. That left victims with different options depending on where they lived, and, in some cases, reliant on platforms to act voluntarily. But Hilton says that current federal law sometimes "doesn't work," leaving victims in need of another way to hold perpetrators accountable. Other critics of the Take It Down Act's shortcomings have pointed to the reliance on federal prosecutors and lack of separate civil remedies for victims to pursue action.

The Sexual Violence Prevention Association, which helped author the Defiance Act, delivered letters to House Speaker Mike Johnson and House Majority Leader Steve Scalise on Tuesday, urging them to bring the bill to the House floor.

"I wrote the Defiance Act because survivors came to me with nowhere to go. Then, I was deepfaked by someone attempting to silence my advocacy for the Defiance Act. I was deepfaked while fighting for the very bill that would prevent it. Suddenly, I too had nowhere to go," said Omny Miranda Martone, founder and CEO of the SVPA, in a statement on Tuesday. "Now it's time for House leadership to say yes."

"The Defiance Act was designed to close those gaps. Once passed, it will help prevent digital sexual violence by giving survivors a way to hold perpetrators accountable and by ending the lack of consequences that emboldens them," Martone stated.

According to the SVPA, the Defiance Act has received 117 endorsements, including Microsoft and Snap, along with advocacy organizations such as National Organization for Women, Center for Democracy and Technology, and Center for AI Policy. In June 2026, OpenAI became the first AI company to endorse it, with Chris Lehane, the company's chief global affairs officer, calling it "smart legislation," and pushing for the House to pass it.

The Senate has passed versions of the bill twice, in 2024 and again in January 2026. Sens. Dick Durbin, the now-deceased Sen. Lindsey Graham (R-SC), Sen. Amy Klobuchar (D-Minn.) and Sen. Josh Hawley (R-MO) were among the original backers. Rep. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez introduced the bipartisan House companion in May 2025, when it was referred to the House Judiciary Committee. It has never received a vote in the House.

In September, House Speaker Mike Johnson told reporters the bill was "good policy" but said the House had yet to build consensus around it. Some Republicans were reportedly reluctant to advance the measure this year because passage would hand Ocasio-Cortez a legislative win ahead of the midterm elections, according to a Politico report from July. Specific objections from House members have not been publicly shared.

The bill currently has 70 House cosponsors, evenly split between 35 Democrats and 35 Republicans.

If the House doesn't act before the current Congress ends, supporters would have to reintroduce the bill in the next Congress. Its path in January will depend on the new makeup of the House and whether its Democratic leaders make the measure a priority. The Democrats are projected to hold a majority in the new Congress, though its exact size is not yet known.

Follow and listen to CNBC Changemakers: Women Leading in the Age of AI wherever you get your podcasts.

If you are having suicidal thoughts or are in distress, contact the Suicide & Crisis Lifeline at 988 for support and assistance from a trained counselor.

#145. We couldn't afford a house alone, so we bought one with strangers

The Pullman family, on the left, and the Wilcox family, bought a home together soon after meeting

When Elizabeth and Tom Wilcox realised they could never afford to buy the home they wanted for themselves and their two children, they decided to take a leap of faith.

They'd find another couple with young kids, complete strangers to them, and propose that they go halves on a property and all move in together.

Based in Vancouver, which has long had some of the highest house prices in Canada, the Wilcoxes were desperate to buy a home that had a back garden for their children to play in.

"We wanted a house with a yard that could fit a trampoline, in a walkable neighbourhood, but we couldn't afford it," says Elizabeth, a project manager in the healthcare sector.

With a budget of C$1m ($700,000; Â£500,000) all they could hope for was a small apartment with a balcony at best.

That was when Elizabeth came up with the idea of buying with another family.

They placed an advert in a local estate agent's e-newsletter, but it took two years before they finally met a family whom they clicked with â Ashley Pullman and her husband and two children.

In 2023, the Wilcoxes and the Pullmans co-bought a six-bedroom, detached house, complete with a back garden, for C$1.85m.

The two families split the deposit of C$250,000, and continue to go halves on the monthly mortgage payments of C$3,900. They also divide bills and other expenses equally, such as any necessary home repairs.

Elizabeth says the arrangement has saved both families "tens of thousands of dollars".

Mums Ashley Pullman, left, and Elizabeth Wilcox say the two families get along

While there is no official data available for the number of people buying homes with people they previously didn't know, Parimal Gosai says he has seen a big increase in interest in Toronto as a response to high property prices.

Gosai is the founder and CEO of Husmates, an app and website that connects people in Canada's largest city who are looking to potentially buy a home with a stranger.

It can be seen as "a financial relationship, and that takes emotions out of it", he says.

It's unclear how common this phenomenon is elsewhere in the world. David Hollingworth, from British mortgage broker L&C, says while he hasn't come across it himself, he thinks it also happens in the UK, though is "very niche".

"I remember that before Covid-19 there was a matchmaking event in a London pub, like speed dating, for people looking to buy a house with a stranger," he says. "How successful this was I don't know."

The Wilcoxes and the Pullmans pooled their resources to buy a detached Vancouver home

Three years on, the Wilcoxes and the Pullmans say their living arrangement is still working out.

While each family has some private living spaces, such as their own kitchens, they share the big basement-level living room, which includes a home gym in one corner, and a shared freezer.

"If someone says they're in the basement to work out, we know not to go there for a bit, and it's never been an issue," says Elizabeth.

Ashley agrees that there are no problems. "Maybe it's arrogance but my husband and I felt like we were the type of people who could live with strangers," she says.

Sabrina Bowman, a freelance business consultant in Toronto, has co-owned a home with two other housemates since 2018. When she signed on the dotted line and moved in she didn't know one of the other two people.

While Sabrina says she isn't comfortable sharing the exact financial details, she says they decided to divide up the deposit and mortgage payments based on each of their levels of income.

She says any household disputes can be addressed early with open discussions. "What is important to us is having structures to communicate about everything that happens in the home."

They have regular house meetings where they discuss chores, financial issues, birthday celebrations, and any disagreements.

"It's great to see people be honest about something they may not be comfortable about and for us to find a solution to that problem," Sabrina says.

Sabrina Bowman says it is important to have open communication

In the case of both Sabrina's house in Toronto and the Wilcox and Pullman property in Vancouver, they have legal agreements in place in case someone wants to sell up and move out.

For both homes, reasonable notice periods are specified, and for Sabrina's, the departing housemate is required to give those remaining the first opportunity to buy their share.

Toronto real estate lawyer Tanya Walker says such legal assurances are vital. "You need something legally binding instead of a bunch of emails and Facebook messages that can be too messy to collect if a dispute comes up," Walker says.

Back in Vancouver, Elizabeth says that neither family has a timeframe for how long they will live in the house. "All of us are really happy with the set-up and location so it would be hard to move.

"I can't put a price on how it felt to watch the kids jump on the trampoline for the first time, or run in circles on our patch of green grass."

#142. We can't afford to hire under-25s, say firms. Will Milburn plans help?

Lowering employers' National Insurance (NI) payments for those between 21 and 24, and cash incentives to take on some workers are among proposals put forward in Alan Milburn's report to get more 18-24 year-olds working.

Many companies have complained that recent increases in National Insurance and the minimum wage have made it more expensive to take on staff.

We talked to firms and business groups about the issues they face when hiring younger workers and what they make of the proposals.

Bournemouth-based cafÃ© owner Ben Blades says he cannot justify the extra cost of training up new staff

Cutting NI costs is "right in the sweet spot of what firms need to hit the hire button", said the Recruitment and Employment Confederation (REC).

The Milburn report proposes NI thresholds could be raised over the next five years, from Â£5,000 to Â£10,000 for those aged 21 to 24, and suggests prioritising the retail and hospitality sectors.

Bournemouth-based cafÃ© owner Ben Blades says the high costs he faces currently make it difficult to employ young people with no work experience.

"My labour costs are already over 45%. We simply cannot justify the extra cost to put on an extra staff member to train someone up for three months," he says.

"In hospitality our margins have been squeezed and squeezed and squeezed"

As a result, he says it currently makes more sense to employ someone who already has experience.

Major report proposes welfare changes, apprenticeships and tax cuts to help 440,000 young people into work

At a glance: Key proposals from Milburn's Neets report

Allen Simpson, chief executive of UKHospitality, says the recommendation to raise the threshold is "the right one" and "would be an immediate shot in the arm for job creation".

But the British Chambers of Commerce (BCC) argues the Milburn report does not go far enough in tackling costs facing businesses.

It says while the NI proposal is welcome, the change should be wider than just retail and hospitality, otherwise it "risks adding complexity and blocking off potential career paths".

Ally Fir says if a state scheme was running she'd "jump on it right away"

Other proposals put forward by the Milburn review include cash incentives to employ younger workers.

Employers who take on any young person who has been Neet - not in education, employment or training - for six months should receive a Â£3,000 payment, and small businesses - with less than 50 employees - that do so should receive an extra Â£2,000

There would also be an extra Â£2,000 payment if the young person has been in care or is disabled.

Ally Fir runs a bookshop in Westbourne, alongside her partner Matt, and thinks more state support is needed.

She says incentivised schemes for small business "would help us to employ more, especially young people. If a scheme like that was going we'd jump on it right away."

Fir says she gets job applications "every single day". She'd like to take more people on but "it is the cost of employing people that makes that impossible".

Tom Parsons has already had experience of using government schemes to employ young people at his design consultancy, Aetha, in Poole, Dorset.

The Kickstart Scheme in 2020 meant he could take on two young people in fully funded six-month job placements offering 25 hours a week.

Tom Parsons says the old Kickstart Scheme was a game-changer for those employed under it

"One of them stayed with us for five years and the other one I think about three years. And it completely changed the game for them," Tom told the BBC. "So if there was a scheme like that, again, that would really help us."

He says he wants to employ young people but costs remain a huge barrier.

Where are the most jobs for 18-24 year-olds?

Why Milburn's Neets reforms will only save Â£200m a year by 2036

One of the government's flagship employment rights reforms risks weakening the hiring of young people, according to Milburn's report.

Ministers are abolishing what they call ''exploitative'' zero-hours contracts and replacing them with guaranteed hours contracts which would be based on the number of hours people normally work.

Much of the detail is yet to be finalised but the Employment Rights Act is due to be enacted next year.

Milburn is concerned that the changes could cause problems "at a time when the youth labour market is already weak'.'

The REC said companies were "particularly worried" over the guaranteed hours measure.

It added that addressing the Neets problem gave ministers "the perfect opportunity" to reconsider the issue.

Young people should have a legal right to a route into education, training or work, and would be supported to do so under an "opportunity guarantee", Milburn has proposed.

This could help firms which are struggling to recruit. Thirty-five-year-old Dean Greene, a sales director at waste management facility Williams Environmental, says it's tricky finding staff.

"We didn't get many applicants for it at all," says Greene, referencing an ad for a marketing and sales role.

"There wasn't a huge uptake from that sort of 16-to-24 range," he adds.

He thinks the lack of applicants may be because the waste industry is perceived as "mucky and dirty", but he is confident many would be interested if they knew the job was available.

Pointing to himself as an example of progressing in the industry, he tells me how he started as an admin officer scanning paperwork at 18 before remaining with the company for 17 years.

Share your experiences and ask your questions.

#140. Fed’s Waller says interest-rate hikes may be needed to ‘shock’ inflation out of the system

The central bank might need to hike interest rates by a full percentage point or more to cool down inflation, Federal Reserve governor Christopher Waller said Wednesday.

→ Baca artikel asli

#139. Where are the most jobs for 18-24 year-olds?

If you're one of the nearly one million people aged 16-24 not in education, employment or training - otherwise known as "Neet" - then you might be wondering where to start.

Under-25s should be guaranteed job placements or training to help them find permanent work, a major review aimed at cutting the number of Neets will say.

But how many jobs are there where you live? Use our look-up tool to find out.

Burnham wants Neets report to be watershed moment, No 10 says, as Tories criticise welfare and tax proposals

Where you live will often have a big impact on your ability to find a job.

The Institute for Public Policy Research (IPPR) think tank used online job adverts to help identify the number of vacancies suitable for young people in the area they live, relative to the size of the local population.

It used AI to help identify the jobs, which were broadly defined as roles requiring relatively low experience or education.

According to its analysis, South Oxfordshire had 387 suitable vacancies per 1,000 18 to 24-year-olds in the area - the most of any local authority. Rotherham had the least, at 53 per 1,000.

Use this tool to check out the situation in your area. The data for London is only available for the region as a whole and is not broken down by individual councils.

Some people live in one area and work in another, but the analysis did not account for this.

Even if there are jobs available in your area, you might not find one in the sector you want to work in.

The sector with the highest average number of entry-level vacancies in the year to October was teaching, with most of those jobs being for teaching assistants, learning support and cover supervisor roles, according to jobs website Adzuna.

The next largest was hospitality and catering, traditionally an area where younger people get their first experience of work.

Adzuna, a major online jobs website, includes adverts for full-time roles marked as entry-level, those asking for up to two years' experience, graduate roles, internships and apprenticeships.

Accounting and finance had the third-highest number of job openings.

Figures on salaries for vacancies can be patchy because many employers do not include them in job adverts.

Prime Minister Andy Burnham recently urged businesses to start publishing salaries ahead of plans to make pay details compulsory in adverts.

Only about 40% of Adzuna's jobs data for the year to October stated a salary, although that figure varies widely by sector.

Here are the average salaries for jobs in the sectors with the largest number of entry-level jobs, according to Adzuna. The highest average salary is Â£30,530 in engineering, while social care has the lowest at Â£25,880.

While it can be hard for anyone looking for their first job, the situation is also tough for older jobseekers.

Across the whole economy there were 690,000 jobs available in the August to October period this year, which is the lowest number since early 2021.

The Covid pandemic led to huge swings in the number of job vacancies available as companies reacted to shutdowns and then reopened as restrictions were lifted.

Stripping out the fluctuations seen during the pandemic, the number of vacancies is now at its lowest level for 12 years.

Why Milburn's Neets reforms will only save Â£200m a year by 2036

Apprenticeships have often been a way for people to get their first taste of employment.

In England the number of people starting apprenticeships has risen in recent years, although this has been driven by an increase in those aged 25 and over after schemes for older workers were fully rolled out in 2008.

The number of people aged 24 and under starting apprenticeships in England in 2025 was 172,230, down from a peak of 291,300 in 2012.

#137. Democrat Mary Peltola wins Alaska Senate election

Democratic candidate Mary Peltola won the Alaska Senate election, capping a midterm cycle that already was one of the best for Democrats in more than a half-century, the Associated Press reported Tuesday evening.

Peltola defeated incumbent Republican Sen. Dan Sullivan in a race that was surrounded by controversy over an independent candidate who shared his name.

Peltola had faced accusations that her campaign recruited ultra-longshot candidate Dan J. Sullivan to the race to confuse voters. The Justice Department filed a lawsuit seeking to delay certification of the election if the incumbent Sullivan lost in order to conduct an investigation into whether voter confusion tainted the outcome.

The Justice Department is appealing the dismissal of that civil suit. The department is also conducting a criminal investigation into the second Sullivan's decision to enter the race.

Peltola's victory gives Democrats a solid majority of 56 seats in the Senate beginning in January.

Democrats this cycle flipped nine Senate seats that are currently held by Republicans.

That outcome, which was not considered remotely likely at the beginning of the election, came as part of a massive Democratic wave this year, which saw the party also flip majority control of the House of Representatives by a decisive margin.

Alaska was the last Senate race to be called in the midterm elections. Peltola, a former House member, joins Senator-elect Peggy Flanagan as the first two Native American women elected to the Senate.

The race was called two weeks after Election Day, and six days after Peltola's fellow Democrat Scott Colom pulled off perhaps the most stunning upset of the 2026 cycle by defeating incumbent Republican Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith in the Mississippi Senate race.

As of 10:39 p.m. ET, Peltola had 50.1% of the vote, and Sullivan had 45.8%. Another Republican, Gerald Heikes, had 2.1% of the vote, while Daniel J. Sullivan had 1.6% of the vote.

If no candidate exceeded 50% of the vote, the election would have been decided through a ranked-choice method, in which the two bottom finishers would have been eliminated, and the second choices on the ballots of voters who selected them would be allocated to the remaining two candidates.

"From Juneau to Utqiagvik, the message from Alaskans is clear: the status quo in DC is not working for Alaska," Peltola said in a statement.

"Over the course of this campaign I've spoken with elders in tears about empty fish racks; families struggling to find housing they can afford; and parents in the Bush faced with the impossible choice of heating their homes or putting food on the table," Peltola said. "Today we take a monumental step forward in addressing those issues and putting Alaska first."As a lifelong Alaskan who has dedicated my life to serving our state, there is no greater honor than having Alaskans' support in our fight to take on corporate greed, lower costs, and put Alaska first," she said. "Thank you Alaska. Let's get to work."

#136. FAA pauses installation of new routers after ground stop

The Federal Aviation Administration said Tuesday it would pause the installation of new routers after problems with the technology led the agency to issue a rare nationwide ground stop on Friday.

Tens of thousands of flights were affected by the ground stop, which halted departing flights for more than four hours. It was the longest halt to U.S. flight departures since 9/11.

Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said Sunday that an overloaded router installed in southern Arkansas as part of an upgrade to a system overseen by L3Harris Technologies appeared to overwhelm systems that feed radar and other data for air traffic control.

The Wall Street Journal first reported the news about the pause.

The FAA and Department of Transportation have said L3Harris was working to convert the communications network from copper wires to fiber optic.

Democratic members of the Senate Committee on Commerce, Science and Transportation this week asked the FAA and the Department of Transportation for clarity on what went wrong.

"Identifying the device that failed does not establish why it failed," the senators wrote in a statement. "Modernization cannot come at the expense of operational safety."

The agencies have said they are working to figure out the root cause of the issue and have reiterated that there is no indication of a cyberattack or outside threat.

The senators also asked for a briefing with the FAA, L3Harris and Juniper Networks, which is making the new devices, by Monday. They asked the companies to lay out why the operational systems lacked sufficient redundancy and answer other questions about what led to the outage.

CNBC has reached out to L3Harris and Juniper Networks for comment.

Duffy on Friday called for Congress to approve $50 billion in funding for upgrades to the aging U.S. air traffic control system, more than he has previously asked for. Democratic lawmakers pushed back on the request, citing unspent money from existing funding, which includes Congress' approval last year of $12.5 billion for airport and air traffic control improvements over five years.

Senate Commerce Chair Ted Cruz, R-Texas, said Tuesday that he thinks there will be support for air traffic control modernization, but that the allocation should be specifically for safety upgrades.

"I don't think the full $50 billion will be funded," he said. "Some of that is not safety related, but we just discussed there, and what I expressed is that the request needs to be focused on safety modernization."

Duffy dismissed the idea that the router issue had to do with the introduction of artificial intelligence tools and said it was "completely unfounded" because the AI system is not in communication with the defective router. He added that the FBI is helping to investigate the root cause of the issue.

Aging equipment and a shortage of air traffic controllers have for years angered airline executives, who have complained about the cost of resulting disruptions.

— CNBC's Phil LeBeau, Leslie Josephs and Justin Papp contributed to this report.

#135. As Trump and Xi meet, China's AI dreams face an unexpected hurdle: the Nvidia spy scandal

Jensen Huang is out but his shadow looms over the upcoming meeting between US President Donald Trump and his Chinese counterpart Xi Jinping this week.

For Beijing, the former Nvidia CEO's dramatic fall is a major challenge - and not just because of claims that a Chinese spy may have honey-trapped Huang, one of the artificial intelligence industry's most influential figures.

Rather, the allegations surrounding Huang and his affair with Chinese businesswoman Alice Huang are turning into a new flashpoint in an already fragile US-China relationship.

US intelligence reports claim she had ties to Beijing's intelligence apparatus - in particular the Ministry of State Security, China's main civilian spy agency.

The claim remains unproven, but it has already reignited debate in Washington over exports of advanced chips to China and whether the Trump administration has been too willing to ease restrictions.

For years now, advanced computer chips have been a key battleground in the US-China rivalry because they are essential for AI, supercomputing and military technology.

Chinese officials are worried this scandal could strengthen voices in Washington who argue in favour of further limiting Chinese access to cutting-edge American tech.

Just this week, the Trump administration introduced new export restrictions on Chinese firms that access advanced US chips in third countries such as Malaysia, Thailand or Brazil. They typically do this by renting computing power in data centres abroad to train their AI models. But some of the firms, like the one Alice Huang worked for, are also accused of smuggling chips to China, although they have denied this.

Until now, this controversy appeared to be a sideshow in US-China relations. But with Beijing calling the new rules "economic coercion" on the eve of Trump arriving in Shenzhen, there is a growing risk it could become something much bigger.

Watch: Jensen Huang speaks to the BBC's Faisal Islam in 2025

Democrats, who will control Congress come January, have made their position clear. Last Wednesday top Democrat Ted Lieu told Politico the Trump administration had been "1,000%" too deferential to Nvidia, putting national security at risk, while fellow Democrat Zoe Lofgren suggested Congress would now be more likely to restrict sales of AI chips to China.

The BBC has approached the White House for comment.

Beijing's foreign ministry had described the spy claims as a "smear against China".

"The US should stop fabricating so-called espionage narratives to find pretexts for imposing technological blockades," spokesperson Mao Ning said last week.

The ministry also seemed keen to keep the meeting on track, saying the two countries had kept "close communication regarding interactions between the two heads of state".

For the last year, Trump and Xi have been seeking to stabilise relations after a damaging tariff war that almost brought trade between the two countries to a standstill.

The Trump administration's decision to permit sales of advanced Nvidia H200 chips to China in late 2025 was seen as evidence that both sides were searching for areas of co-operation, even while competition intensified.

It was a rare move given Washington had spent years trying to limit China's access to the most advanced chips.

Many in the US have long viewed these chips as a national security issue, arguing they could strengthen China's military and surveillance capabilities. Jensen Huang, who rejects that claim, advocated lifting bans on the export of high-performing chips to China. Beijing argues the restrictions are an attempt to contain its rise.

History has taught Beijing and Washington that spy scandals rarely remain confined to the intelligence world.

Analysts in China already see echoes of an incident in 2023 when a suspected surveillance balloon drifted through the skies over the US before an American fighter jet shot it down. Beijing insisted it was a civilian meteorological airship that had strayed off course.

The suspected Chinese spy balloon was shot down days after it was spotted over the US

But a planned trip by then US Secretary of State Antony Blinken was cancelled and communication between the two governments deteriorated sharply.

The incident showed how quickly a security dispute could overwhelm diplomatic efforts and push relations into a deep freeze.

"The mere passage of a balloon sent American public opinion and politicians into a state of high alert, as if a "Chinese threat" were looming directly overhead," said the state media outlet, the Global Times this week as it made a direct comparison with the Nvidia scandal.

"No-one on the US side has ever apologised or taken responsibility for the genuine damage this caused to US-China relations," it added.

The state-run outlet also questioned why the Nvidia story had emerged so soon after "the hard-won detente" between Beijing and Washington, following a meeting between the two countries' leaders in September.

"Is someone behind the scenes pushing an agenda and stirring up confrontation?" it asked.

Both Trump and Xi have significant incentives to keep talking.

China's economy remains under pressure and is increasingly reliant on exports. Xi needs trade to keep flowing and this meeting is a key stepping stone to replacing the current trade truce that is due to expire in January.

Meanwhile Trump has repeatedly touted his close relationship with Xi and argued that maintaining dialogue is essential to managing competition between the two powers.

The US president is in his last two years and faces an uphill struggle to pass domestic legislation after the midterms. To counter this, he could be looking for some foreign policy wins.

He may also hope for Xi's help to end the war in the Middle East.

Neither side will want to be discussing intelligence concerns and technology controls instead of trade, investment and economic co-operation.

The summit is also being carefully choreographed by Beijing to show off its technological prowess in Shenzhen - a deeply symbolic choice of city.

It's home to some of the country's biggest tech companies and has become a showcase for Beijing's ambitions in AI and advanced manufacturing.

Also, given Washington's tighter restrictions on advanced chips, Beijing has options for retaliation.

One of its most powerful tools is its dominance of key rare earth minerals. China processes much of the world's supply for materials that are used in everything from electric vehicles and smartphones to military equipment.

Analysts have long warned that technological restrictions can trigger a cycle of retaliation in which each side reaches for its strongest economic leverage.

That would shift the current trend of US-China relations from engagement back towards mutual suspicion.

#134. Waymo pulls driverless taxis from all US freeways

Waymo has temporarily suspended its driverless vehicles from operating on all US freeways, the company has said.

The decision comes after California banned Waymo's driverless taxis from state freeways following a series of recent traffic incidents.

California's Department of Motor Vehicles (DMV) cited an "imminent hazard to public safety" for the ban, noting an incident this month in which several driverless Waymo vehicles entered an active construction zone on US Route 101.

Waymo said it was working with state officials "diligently and responsibly to resume freeway operations", adding that no injuries or collisions were reported following the incident on US Route 101 south of San Francisco.

California's DMV said Waymo's driverless taxis presented a risk "due to recurring incursions by its vehicles into active construction zones where construction workers, vehicles and equipment are present".

In another incident earlier this month, footage widely seen on social media appeared to show a Waymo car trying to overtake patrol vehicles that were deliberately slowing the pace of traffic on a San Francisco highway.

The California ban, which came into effect on 11 November, would remain in place until Waymo "demonstrates to the department that it has taken effective corrective actions to address the deficiencies that precipitated the restrictions".

A Waymo spokesperson said: "In alignment with our safety-first principles, we have also temporarily restricted freeway operations across our other operating markets to evaluate our performance."

Data on Waymo's website suggests that its cars, compared to human drivers, are involved in 95% fewer crashes resulting in serious injury.

Waymo currently operates driverless taxis in 15 US cities, but only offered freeway rides in a handful of those locations, including San Francisco, Los Angeles and Miami.

The company's driverless taxis can continue to operate on regular city streets.

In May, Waymo recalled thousands of its self-driving cars in the US over a software issue that could allow vehicles to drive into flooded roads.

In the same month, multiple Waymo cars were spotted driving around a cul-de-sac in Atlanta, Georgia, in the early hours of the morning.

#133. Jensen Huang has agreed to appear before Senate banking committee, Chairman Scott says

Former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang has agreed to appear before the Senate Banking Committee, panel Chair Tim Scott, R-S.C., said on CNBC's "Squawk Box" on Wednesday.

Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick has also agreed to testify, Scott said. The agreements to appear voluntarily come as pressure builds on Capitol Hill to investigate Huang's affair with a suspected Chinese spy, which led to his ouster at the chipmaker he founded.

"They both have agreed," Scott said. "We will hopefully have this done before Christmas."

Huang reached an agreement on Tuesday with the chairmen of three House committees for a closed-door transcribed interview on Dec. 1 jointly before the three panels. Scott had asked Huang to appear publicly in the Senate.

Scott said the appearances will provide the committee with "more information about what they knew, when they knew it and why they didn't do something about it."

The outgoing banking committee chair also homed in on Lutnick, calling for answers as to why the commerce secretary did not act after being warned about Megaspeed — the Singaporean company where the alleged Chinese spy, Alice Huang, was an executive. Nvidia sold its chips to Megaspeed, which U.S. investigators suspect of diverting them to China.

"They were warned as early as March of this year, several months ago, that Megaspeed should be on the 'entity list,'" Scott said, referring to a Commerce Department list of companies the U.S. views as national security risks. The Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security "didn't do it, Howard didn't do it, we want to know why."

Lutnick and Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent have been embroiled in an intra-administration battle as the fallout from the Huang affair grows. Bessent on CNBC on Friday appeared to blame Lutnick for a lack of action on Megaspeed.

"You will have to ask them about their internal processes," Bessent said, referring to Commerce and noting his department has long supported imposing restrictions on Megaspeed.

A spokesperson for Huang declined to comment on Scott's announcement that he has agreed to appear.

Huang's upcoming appearances are likely just the start of the embattled former CEO's problems on Capitol Hill. Democrats will take control of Congress in January and have demanded sweeping investigations into the affair and Huang's cozy relationship with President Donald Trump.

#132. Security flaw found in Java, world's third-largest programming language

A tech start-up used AI to uncover a critical software bug in Java that had existed for more than a decade (generic photo)

A cyber-security firm has used artificial intelligence (AI) to uncover a critical flaw in the hugely popular computer programming language Java.

Tech start-up Calif - which recently identified a cyber-security flaw in another language, PHP - found the bug in Java's memory functions.

Java is a popular programming language used by nearly all of the world's top companies such as Apple and Amazon, as well as many banks, hospitals and governments.

It is meant to make the IT world more secure by managing your memory online, said computer scientist Professor Toby Walsh, which is why "this is such an unlikely and serious flaw".

The discovery comes after a string of serious technology issues, including a massive Microsoft outage.

On Tuesday, the US National Security Agency said its internal communications went offline on Tuesday due to a "scheduled routine maintenance".

The NSA did not specify whether its outage was linked to the identified Java bug.

Apple also reported multiple services suffered full outages due to an internal update, while a network failure grounded all flights in the US.

US Secretary of State Marco Rubio said there was "zero evidence" the incidents were related.

With the help of AI, Calif discovered a serious security vulnerability called Java4Shell, which allowed an adversary to remotely execute unwanted commands to Java's memory system.

In this case, the adversary was OpenAI's recently-released model GPT-6 Cyber, which is not yet available to the public.

But there are fears hackers could now identify the bug if the flaw isn't fixed.

Oracle, the owner of Java, has not acknowledged the bug, but released a "critical patch update" on Tuesday, which it said contained security vulnerability fixes.

At this stage it is unclear if any platforms or companies have been affected by the latest incident.

But the Java flaw remains a huge concern for cyber-security experts, who say overloading a system's memory is one of the most common ways for hackers to exploit or crash a system.

The BBC has contacted Oracle for comment.

What is PHP and why did it spark a global cyber-security scramble?

US National Security Agency's classified internal network reportedly goes offline

Nearly every critical sector, including banking, national security systems, aviation and telecommunications, relies on Java.

First released in 1995, the programming language and computing system is now the "beating heart of the modern economy", Calif said in its investigation.

One of its main functions is to manage and protect memory for you, Walsh, a leading researcher in AI, told the BBC.

Any time you transfer information on the internet, receive a file from a user or need to log into an IT system with a password, the platform you are using is likely relying on a Java program.

The serious flaw could comprise "critical financial systems, customers and organisation's data," cyber-security expert Dr Pedram Hayati said.

In Australia alone, almost every bank uses Java for managing critical transaction data, Hayati added.

The latest incidents have exposed how nearly every critical sector - including national security systems, aviation and telecommunications - relies on legacy infrastructure online.

Earlier this week, thousands of customers at major US banks, including JP Morgan Chase, Wells Fargo and Bank of America, reported trouble accessing services.

The Java discovery follows a critical cyber-security flaw identified in the PHP programming language, also identified by Calif.

PHP is the code that websites like Wikipedia use to collect information and present it in a way you can read on the web.

Surrey Centre for Cyber Security Prof Alan Woodward said the PHP flaw affected some 30 million websites, leaving them vulnerable to malicious attacks.

At the time, website owners were urged to check if their sites were affected and, if so, they were told to install updates to patch the issue or consider going offline.

The PHP flaws went undetected for at least a decade, which Walsh said is similar to what has happened with Java.

"It's been hiding in plain sight for 12 years", he said.

"AI is now exposing more flaws much more quickly," Walsh said including ones that may have been so obscure and difficult that humans could not have discovered them alone.

Earlier in November, OpenAI president Greg Brockman announced his company was pivoting 25% of its staff to focus on cyber-security demands.

Are we too reliant on big tech companies?

#131. We need to get older people back into work too

The Milburn review shows the government is taking the Neet crisis seriously. Underemployed over-fifties deserve the same attention

→ Baca artikel asli

#130. Speed camera fault may have allowed drivers to escape fines

Further problems with the accuracy of speed cameras on English roads have been uncovered which may have allowed drivers to slip through the cracks and escape fines.

National Highways discovered issues with speed cameras last year, affecting 154 out of 400 cameras but, following an investigation, said it had found another fault on units monitoring roads including those running between Huntingdon and Cambridge.

The Home Office has revoked approval that allows footage from the cameras to be used as evidence in legal proceedings. US firm Verra Mobility operates the cameras but National Highways said it will now end the contract.

Roads minister Justin Madders said: "We can't take chances with this.

"If we're going to prosecute people, we need to be confident the system is working as it should. That's why we've taken the action we have."

In a statement, Verra Mobility said it "remains fully committed to delivering reliable, innovative technology that helps make roads safer".

The fault affected all of the variable speed cameras on smart motorways, and a section of the A14 between Huntingdon and Cambridge as well as the A1 approach junction to the A14.

A spokesperson for National Highways said that Verra's camera system had been selected on the basis that it maintained Home Office approval.

The agency said that since that was no longer the case "we are ending our contractual relationship to use the cameras for enforcement".

It added: "We are considering all further commercial and legal options that may be appropriate."

The BBC has contacted Verra Mobility for comment.

Madders said that the enforcement of speed limits continues to be conducted through a range of different tactics, including mobile cameras as well as marked and unmarked police patrols.

But he admitted that the camera system will not be fully fixed until sometime next year.

Rod Dennis, senior policy officer at the RAC motoring organisation, said: "Malfunctioning speed cameras on smart motorways do more than just let speeding drivers get away with breaking the law and endangering lives â they further undermine confidence in the very types of road we know drivers already have serious misgivings about."

There are 4,000 cameras across the entire road network.

In December 2025, National Highways, which runs England's motorways, blamed an "anomaly" in how variable speed cameras were interacting with speed limit signs on some A roads and motorways.

For example, on a road where the speed limit increases, a driver may see a sign showing 60mph but the camera recording it may still be working on the basis of a previous 40mph speed limit.

The fault led to 2,650 wrongful speed camera activations between 2021 and the end of last year.

Those who had been wrongfully fined have been reimbursed.

As National Highways continued to test the system, it discovered a further problem "relating to how some of these cameras may activate in specific and rare circumstances".

It said that according to its calculations, "the circumstances in which the latest issue could occur are extremely rare, and there is no evidence to suggest it has impacted enforcement".

Nevertheless, it said it was "taking immediate steps to ensure compliance is maintained at relevant locations".

It said it would put further traffic officer patrols in place to monitor the sites.

#129. How to get Anthropic stock — without buying the IPO

Anthropic's initial public offering on Thursday may be the largest in history — and everyday investors can get a piece of the stock without buying the IPO directly.

Many actively managed mutual funds and exchange-traded funds already own pre-IPO stock in the artificial intelligence startup — and investors in those funds will automatically get a sliver of Anthropic's IPO when it starts trading. Investors in certain index funds will gain access to the stock within weeks, though timing will vary by fund.

Investors' exposure to Anthropic in mutual funds and ETFs will likely be relatively low — well below 1% of the overall portfolio for most funds, experts said. That dynamic helps investors avoid being too concentrated in any one stock, thereby reducing risk and diluting the volatility that generally accompanies an IPO.

"I would certainly not recommend to anybody putting all of your eggs in one basket with any stock," said Jay Ritter, director of The IPO Initiative at the University of Florida.

It's an important point, as the Anthropic IPO could be the biggest ever.

The company is expected to raise up to roughly $90 billion in its market debut — a sum that would beat current record holder SpaceX, which raised $86 billion in a blockbuster IPO in June.

The IPO comes at a time when some market watchers are questioning if Jensen Huang's recent resignation as CEO of chipmaker Nvidia — following the revelation of his extramarital affairs — threatens to derail the AI boom.

Anthropic won't be available in index funds — which track the returns of a specific market index like the S&P 500 — right when trading starts.

Each market index has different rules that govern when specific stocks become eligible for inclusion.

Some popular indexes will add Anthropic relatively quickly, Ritter said.

One example is the Morningstar U.S. Total Market Index, he said, which will add Anthropic stock after a five-day waiting period following its IPO.

Funds that track this index — such as the $1.8 trillion Vanguard Morningstar Total Stock Market Index Fund (VTI), one of the largest stock ETFs — will likely add Anthropic stock by the end of November, said Brendan McCann, senior associate manager research analyst at Morningstar.

MSCI, another index provider, adds large IPOs after 10 trading days.

Funds that track its indexes — including the iShares MSCI USA ETF (MLRG), which tracks the returns of large- and mid-cap U.S. stocks — should add Anthropic in early December, McCann said.

Index providers like Nasdaq and FTSE Russell earlier this year accelerated their timelines to add large IPOs, to accommodate interest in SpaceX's market debut.

However, in both cases, investors will likely have to wait longer to access Anthropic stock than they did to get SpaceX, experts said

FTSE Russell indexes like the Russell 3000 — which tracks the performance of the 3,000 largest stocks trading on U.S. exchanges — typically have a waiting period of five days to add large IPOs. The iShares Russell 3000 ETF (IWV) and Vanguard Russell 3000 ETF (VTHR) track this index, for example.

However, Anthropic won't be available in FTSE Russell indexes until the market opens on Dec. 14, the provider said on Friday.

That's because the typical five-day window would fall close to Thanksgiving Day, FTSE Russell said. Instead, the provider will wait to add the stock until its semiannual review period in December.

The waiting period will be about three weeks, compared to about one week for SpaceX, McCann said.

Similarly, Nasdaq would typically have a 15-day waiting period to add Anthropic stock to its indexes, such as the Nasdaq-100. This index measures the performance of 100 of the largest non-financial companies listed on the Nasdaq stock exchange, many of them in the technology sector.

That waiting period would normally end on Dec. 11.

However, the Nasdaq-100 won't add Anthropic until after Dec. 18, when Nasdaq completes its periodic review of index constituents, McCann said. The delay is due to Nasdaq's explicit methodology for timing IPO additions.

In both cases, the delays are "nothing about [Anthropic] being inherently riskier than SpaceX," McCann said. "It's completely an IPO timing thing."

The $463 billion Invesco QQQ Trust (QQQ), one of the largest stock ETFs, is an example of a fund that tracks the Nasdaq-100, Ritter said.

Meanwhile, investors in the S&P 500 — perhaps the best-known stock index — will have to wait at least a year for Anthropic to join the ranks.

The provider, S&P Dow Jones, requires companies to be public for at least 12 months to be eligible for inclusion in the S&P 500. Additionally, the company must be profitable.

Anthropic's weighting in specific index funds depends on factors like how high the stock price goes and how the index calculates the "float."

The float is the total number of shares available for public investors to buy and sell, though Ritter said there isn't a uniform definition of which types of shares are included in this calculation.

Ritter estimated Anthropic shares would represent about 0.15% of the Morningstar U.S. Total Market Index. He made this projection on Monday based on various index characteristics and assumptions like a $1.5 trillion Anthropic valuation. Estimates suggest its valuation will be in that ballpark.

The Nasdaq-100 will have a larger weight of about 0.8%, Ritter calculated.

However, that's higher than most, experts said.

"Most [index funds] will only be picking up Anthropic in the neighborhood of 10-basis-point positions," said Stephen Welch, a senior equity strategy analyst at Morningstar. Ten basis points equals 0.1%.

Some actively managed funds have much larger positions, thanks to private Anthropic stock that asset managers purchased before the IPO.

Investors in these funds will have effectively bought the IPO as soon as trading starts.

For example, the Coatue Innovative Strategies Fund had a roughly 15.7% weighting to Anthropic as of June 30, according to the most recent Morningstar data.

This is a closed-end fund, which isn't as liquid as mutual funds or ETFs and generally isn't easily tradable, Welch said.

Everyday investors should largely stick to mutual funds or ETFs, he said.

The Tema Photonics & Optical ETF (LAZR) and Yorkville America 2X Long Mangos Plus Daily Target ETF (MNGZ) have the highest Anthropic weightings of any ETF or mutual fund — about 12.9% and 10.7% of overall fund holdings, respectively, as of early November, according to Morningstar.

However, long-term investors should likely avoid funds that have such concentrated holdings, Welch said.

"I don't think I'd recommend investing in something more specialized like that," he said. "It's a thematic, very niche ETF that won't be very diversified."

Ultimately, owning the "latest hot IPO" shouldn't be investors' primary reason for buying or selling a fund, Welch wrote in October.

"You should evaluate an equity fund on its manager, approach, parent company, and the role it plays in a long-term portfolio, not just on how well it's situated for the next record-breaking public offering," he wrote. "Chasing IPOs with funds can easily backfire."
